# Research: subset-b-000581

Grouped research for Linux devicetree binding schemas covering touchscreen/input, interconnect, and interrupt-controller nodes. Each section preserves the source path and marker pair required for deterministic reconciliation into source-tree-aligned per-file documents.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/fsl,imx25-tcq.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/fsl,imx25-tcq.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Freescale mx25 TS conversion queue module` touchscreen controller binding. mx25 touchscreen conversion queue module which controls the ADC unit of the mx25 for attached touchscreens. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `fsl,imx25-tcq`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `fsl,wires`, `fsl,pen-debounce-ns`, `fsl,pen-threshold`, `fsl,settling-time-ns`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 7 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `compatible`, `fsl,wires`, `interrupts`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Touchscreen/input-facing properties define the hardware contract consumed by Linux input drivers: bus address resources `reg`, IRQ lines `interrupts`, reset or handshake GPIOs none, power/clock/reset resources none, and calibration/input capabilities `fsl,wires`, `fsl,pen-debounce-ns`, `fsl,pen-threshold`, `fsl,settling-time-ns`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `fsl,wires`, which is what makes a DTS node eligible for schema-valid probing.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For input devices, the validated node is then consumed by I2C, SPI, platform, GPIO, regulator, and Linux input subsystem code, which interprets coordinates, axis inversion, gesture keys, wakeup behavior, and panel geometry from the properties admitted by this schema. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `touchscreen.yaml`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Frank Li <Frank.Li@nxp.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include I2C/SPI/platform bus enumeration, GPIO controllers, regulators, interrupt controllers, pinctrl, optional wakeup handling, and the Linux input core.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 7 top-level properties and 7 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/fsl,imx25-tcq.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `fsl,imx25-tcq`. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include boot logs for touchscreen/input driver probe, IRQ trigger sanity, regulator and reset sequencing, coordinate transform behavior, `evtest`/input-event output, and DTS users that exercise optional keycode, wakeup, GPIO, and axis-size properties.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/fsl,imx25-tcq.yaml -->

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/hynitron,cst816x.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/hynitron,cst816x.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Hynitron CST816x Series Capacitive Touch controller` touchscreen controller binding. Bindings for CST816x high performance self-capacitance touch chip series with single point gesture and real two-point operation. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 1 compatible token: `hynitron,cst816s`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `reset-gpios`, `linux,keycodes`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 5 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `compatible`, `interrupts`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Touchscreen/input-facing properties define the hardware contract consumed by Linux input drivers: bus address resources `reg`, IRQ lines `interrupts`, reset or handshake GPIOs `reset-gpios`, power/clock/reset resources `reset-gpios`, and calibration/input capabilities `linux,keycodes`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, which is what makes a DTS node eligible for schema-valid probing.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For input devices, the validated node is then consumed by I2C, SPI, platform, GPIO, regulator, and Linux input subsystem code, which interprets coordinates, axis inversion, gesture keys, wakeup behavior, and panel geometry from the properties admitted by this schema. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are none; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are none.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Oleh Kuzhylnyi <kuzhylol@gmail.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include I2C/SPI/platform bus enumeration, GPIO controllers, regulators, interrupt controllers, pinctrl, optional wakeup handling, and the Linux input core.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 5 top-level properties and 5 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/hynitron,cst816x.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `hynitron,cst816s`. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include boot logs for touchscreen/input driver probe, IRQ trigger sanity, regulator and reset sequencing, coordinate transform behavior, `evtest`/input-event output, and DTS users that exercise optional keycode, wakeup, GPIO, and axis-size properties.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/hynitron,cst816x.yaml -->

## Dependencies and Integration Points

Runtime dependencies commonly include I2C/SPI/platform bus enumeration, GPIO controllers, regulators, interrupt controllers, pinctrl, optional wakeup handling, and the Linux input core.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 8 top-level properties and 8 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Touchscreen/input-facing properties define the hardware contract consumed by Linux input drivers: bus address resources `reg`, IRQ lines `interrupts`, reset or handshake GPIOs `power-gpios`, power/clock/reset resources `avdd-supply`, `vddio-supply`, and calibration/input capabilities none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `power-gpios`, `touchscreen-size-x`, `touchscreen-size-y`, which is what makes a DTS node eligible for schema-valid probing.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For input devices, the validated node is then consumed by I2C, SPI, platform, GPIO, regulator, and Linux input subsystem code, which interprets coordinates, axis inversion, gesture keys, wakeup behavior, and panel geometry from the properties admitted by this schema. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `touchscreen.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Dmitry Torokhov <dmitry.torokhov@gmail.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Runtime dependencies commonly include I2C/SPI/platform bus enumeration, GPIO controllers, regulators, interrupt controllers, pinctrl, optional wakeup handling, and the Linux input core.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 9 top-level properties and 9 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 2 example blocks. Runtime dependencies commonly include I2C/SPI/platform bus enumeration, GPIO controllers, regulators, interrupt controllers, pinctrl, optional wakeup handling, and the Linux input core.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 15 top-level properties and 15 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/ti,tsc2005.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `ti,tsc2004`, `ti,tsc2005`. The 2 example blocks in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include boot logs for touchscreen/input driver probe, IRQ trigger sanity, regulator and reset sequencing, coordinate transform behavior, `evtest`/input-event output, and DTS users that exercise optional keycode, wakeup, GPIO, and axis-size properties.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/ti,tsc2007.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/input/touchscreen/ti,tsc2007.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Texas Instruments tsc2007 touchscreen controller` touchscreen controller binding. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `ti,tsc2007`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `ti,x-plate-ohms`, `gpios`, `pendown-gpio`, `wakeup-source`, `ti,max-rt`, `ti,fuzzx`, `ti,fuzzy`, `ti,fuzzz`, `ti,poll-period`, `#io-channel-cells`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 13 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `compatible`, `reg`, `ti,x-plate-ohms`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Touchscreen/input-facing properties define the hardware contract consumed by Linux input drivers: bus address resources `reg`, IRQ lines `interrupts`, reset or handshake GPIOs `pendown-gpio`, power/clock/reset resources none, and calibration/input capabilities `ti,x-plate-ohms`, `wakeup-source`, `ti,max-rt`, `ti,fuzzx`, `ti,fuzzy`, `ti,fuzzz`, `ti,poll-period`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `ti,x-plate-ohms`, which is what makes a DTS node eligible for schema-valid probing.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For input devices, the validated node is then consumed by I2C, SPI, platform, GPIO, regulator, and Linux input subsystem code, which interprets coordinates, axis inversion, gesture keys, wakeup behavior, and panel geometry from the properties admitted by this schema. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are none.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/fsl,imx8m-noc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/fsl,imx8m-noc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Generic i.MX bus frequency device` interconnect provider binding. The i.MX SoC family has multiple buses for which clock frequency (and sometimes voltage) can be adjusted. Some of those buses expose register areas mentioned in the memory maps as GPV ("Global Programmers View") but not all. Access to this area might be denied for normal (non-secure) world. The buses are based on externally licensed IPs such as ARM NIC-301 and Arteris FlexNOC but DT bindings are specific to the integration of these bus interconnect IPs into imx SOCs. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `oneOf` with 3 accepted compatible forms and covers 10 compatible tokens: `fsl,imx8mm-nic`, `fsl,imx8mn-nic`, `fsl,imx8mp-nic`, `fsl,imx8mq-nic`, `fsl,imx8m-nic`, `fsl,imx8mm-noc`, `fsl,imx8mn-noc`, `fsl,imx8mp-noc`, `fsl,imx8mq-noc`, `fsl,imx8m-noc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `clocks`, `operating-points-v2`, `opp-table`, `fsl,ddrc`, `#interconnect-cells`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 7 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `clocks`, `compatible`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells `#interconnect-cells`, clocks/power/OPP hooks `clocks`, `operating-points-v2`, `opp-table`, and SoC/vendor topology knobs `operating-points-v2`, `#interconnect-cells`. Required properties are `compatible`, `clocks`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `oneOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Peng Fan <peng.fan@nxp.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 7 top-level properties and 7 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/fsl,imx8m-noc.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `fsl,imx8mm-nic`, `fsl,imx8mn-nic`, `fsl,imx8mp-nic`, `fsl,imx8mq-nic`, `fsl,imx8m-nic`, `fsl,imx8mm-noc`, `fsl,imx8mn-noc`, `fsl,imx8mp-noc`, and 2 more. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include successful interconnect provider registration, consumer bandwidth votes, devfreq/OPP transitions, clock and power-domain enablement, and no `dtbs_check` regressions for SoC DTSI files that include the provider topology.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,glymur-rpmh.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,glymur-rpmh.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm RPMh Network-On-Chip Interconnect on Glymur and Mahua SoCs` Qualcomm RPMh interconnect provider binding. RPMh interconnect providers support system bandwidth requirements through RPMh hardware accelerators known as Bus Clock Manager (BCM). The provider is able to communicate with the BCM through the Resource State Coordinator (RSC) associated with each execution environment. Provider nodes must point to at least one RPMh device child node pertaining to their RSC and each provider can map to multiple RPMh resources. See also: include/dt-bindings/interconnect/qcom,glymur-rpmh.h It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `oneOf` with 17 accepted compatible forms and covers 42 compatible tokens: `qcom,mahua-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,mahua-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,mahua-aggre3-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre3-noc`, `qcom,mahua-aggre4-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre4-noc`, `qcom,mahua-clk-virt`, `qcom,glymur-clk-virt`, `qcom,mahua-cnoc-main`, `qcom,glymur-cnoc-main`, `qcom,mahua-lpass-ag-noc`, `qcom,glymur-lpass-ag-noc`, `qcom,mahua-lpass-lpiaon-noc`, `qcom,glymur-lpass-lpiaon-noc`, `qcom,mahua-lpass-lpicx-noc`, `qcom,glymur-lpass-lpicx-noc`, `qcom,mahua-mmss-noc`, `qcom,glymur-mmss-noc`, and 22 more. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `clocks`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `clocks`, `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells none, clocks/power/OPP hooks `clocks`, and SoC/vendor topology knobs none. Required properties are `compatible`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpmh-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `else`, `if`, `oneOf`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Raviteja Laggyshetty <raviteja.laggyshetty@oss.qualcomm.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 3 top-level properties and 3 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,glymur-rpmh.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `qcom,mahua-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,mahua-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,mahua-aggre3-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre3-noc`, `qcom,mahua-aggre4-noc`, `qcom,glymur-aggre4-noc`, and 34 more. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include successful interconnect provider registration, consumer bandwidth votes, devfreq/OPP transitions, clock and power-domain enablement, and no `dtbs_check` regressions for SoC DTSI files that include the provider topology.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,glymur-rpmh.yaml -->

Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells none, clocks/power/OPP hooks none, and SoC/vendor topology knobs none. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpm-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,msm8996.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,msm8996.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm MSM8996 Network-On-Chip interconnect` Qualcomm interconnect provider binding. The Qualcomm MSM8996 interconnect providers support adjusting the bandwidth requirements between the various NoC fabrics. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 8 compatible tokens: `qcom,msm8996-a0noc`, `qcom,msm8996-a1noc`, `qcom,msm8996-a2noc`, `qcom,msm8996-bimc`, `qcom,msm8996-cnoc`, `qcom,msm8996-mnoc`, `qcom,msm8996-pnoc`, `qcom,msm8996-snoc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `clock-names`, `clocks`, `power-domains`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 5 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `compatible`, `power-domains`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells none, clocks/power/OPP hooks `clock-names`, `clocks`, `power-domains`, and SoC/vendor topology knobs none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpm-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `if`, `then`.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 5 top-level properties and 5 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,msm8998-bwmon.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,msm8998-bwmon.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm Interconnect Bandwidth Monitor` Qualcomm interconnect provider binding. Bandwidth Monitor measures current throughput on buses between various NoC fabrics and provides information when it crosses configured thresholds. Certain SoCs might have more than one Bandwidth Monitors, for example on SDM845:: - Measuring the bandwidth between CPUs and Last Level Cache Controller - called just BWMON, - Measuring the bandwidth between Last Level Cache Controller and memory (DDR) - called LLCC BWMON. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,qcs615-rpmh.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,qcs615-rpmh.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm RPMh Network-On-Chip Interconnect on QCS615` Qualcomm RPMh interconnect provider binding. RPMh interconnect providers support system bandwidth requirements through RPMh hardware accelerators known as Bus Clock Manager (BCM). The provider is able to communicate with the BCM through the Resource State Coordinator (RSC) associated with each execution environment. Provider nodes must point to at least one RPMh device child node pertaining to their RSC and each provider can map to multiple RPMh resources. See also: include/dt-bindings/interconnect/qcom,qcs615-rpmh.h It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,qdu1000-rpmh.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,qdu1000-rpmh.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm RPMh Network-On-Chip Interconnect on QDU1000` Qualcomm RPMh interconnect provider binding. RPMh interconnect providers support system bandwidth requirements through RPMh hardware accelerators known as Bus Clock Manager (BCM). The provider is able to communicate with the BCM through the Resource State Coordinator (RSC) associated with each execution environment. Provider nodes must point to at least one RPMh device child node pertaining to their RSC and each provider can map to multiple RPMh resources. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 4 compatible tokens: `qcom,qdu1000-clk-virt`, `qcom,qdu1000-gem-noc`, `qcom,qdu1000-mc-virt`, `qcom,qdu1000-system-noc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `#interconnect-cells`, `reg`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells `#interconnect-cells`, clocks/power/OPP hooks none, and SoC/vendor topology knobs `#interconnect-cells`. Required properties are `compatible`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpmh-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `else`, `if`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Georgi Djakov <djakov@kernel.org>, Odelu Kukatla <quic_okukatla@quicinc.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 3 top-level properties and 3 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,qdu1000-rpmh.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `qcom,qdu1000-clk-virt`, `qcom,qdu1000-gem-noc`, `qcom,qdu1000-mc-virt`, `qcom,qdu1000-system-noc`. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sar2130p-rpmh.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sar2130p-rpmh.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm RPMh Network-On-Chip Interconnect on SAR2130P` Qualcomm RPMh interconnect provider binding. RPMh interconnect providers support system bandwidth requirements through RPMh hardware accelerators known as Bus Clock Manager (BCM). The provider is able to communicate with the BCM through the Resource State Coordinator (RSC) associated with each execution environment. Provider nodes must point to at least one RPMh device child node pertaining to their RSC and each provider can map to multiple RPMh resources. See also: include/dt-bindings/interconnect/qcom,sar2130p-rpmh.h It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 9 compatible tokens: `qcom,sar2130p-clk-virt`, `qcom,sar2130p-config-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-gem-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-lpass-ag-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-mc-virt`, `qcom,sar2130p-mmss-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-nsp-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-pcie-anoc`, `qcom,sar2130p-system-noc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `clocks`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `clocks`, `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells none, clocks/power/OPP hooks `clocks`, and SoC/vendor topology knobs none. Required properties are `compatible`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpmh-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `else`, `if`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Dmitry Baryshkov <dmitry.baryshkov@linaro.org>, Georgi Djakov <djakov@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 3 top-level properties and 3 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sar2130p-rpmh.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `qcom,sar2130p-clk-virt`, `qcom,sar2130p-config-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-gem-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-lpass-ag-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-mc-virt`, `qcom,sar2130p-mmss-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-nsp-noc`, `qcom,sar2130p-pcie-anoc`, and 1 more. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include successful interconnect provider registration, consumer bandwidth votes, devfreq/OPP transitions, clock and power-domain enablement, and no `dtbs_check` regressions for SoC DTSI files that include the provider topology.

The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include successful interconnect provider registration, consumer bandwidth votes, devfreq/OPP transitions, clock and power-domain enablement, and no `dtbs_check` regressions for SoC DTSI files that include the provider topology.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sm6350-rpmh.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sm6350-rpmh.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm SM6350 RPMh Network-On-Chip Interconnect` Qualcomm RPMh interconnect provider binding. Qualcomm RPMh-based interconnect provider on SM6350. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 8 compatible tokens: `qcom,sm6350-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-config-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-dc-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-gem-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-mmss-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-npu-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-system-noc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `clocks`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `clocks`, `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells none, clocks/power/OPP hooks `clocks`, and SoC/vendor topology knobs none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpmh-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `else`, `if`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Luca Weiss <luca.weiss@fairphone.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are `^interconnect-[a-z0-9\-]+$`; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 3 top-level properties and 3 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sm6350-rpmh.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `qcom,sm6350-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-config-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-dc-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-gem-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-mmss-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-npu-noc`, `qcom,sm6350-system-noc`. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include successful interconnect provider registration, consumer bandwidth votes, devfreq/OPP transitions, clock and power-domain enablement, and no `dtbs_check` regressions for SoC DTSI files that include the provider topology.

`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sm8550-rpmh.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm RPMh Network-On-Chip Interconnect on SM8550` Qualcomm RPMh interconnect provider binding. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpmh-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `else`, `if`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Abel Vesa <abel.vesa@linaro.org>, Neil Armstrong <neil.armstrong@linaro.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 3 top-level properties and 3 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,sm8750-rpmh.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `qcom,sm8750-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,sm8750-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,sm8750-clk-virt`, `qcom,sm8750-cnoc-main`, `qcom,sm8750-config-noc`, `qcom,sm8750-gem-noc`, `qcom,sm8750-lpass-ag-noc`, `qcom,sm8750-lpass-lpiaon-noc`, and 6 more. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include successful interconnect provider registration, consumer bandwidth votes, devfreq/OPP transitions, clock and power-domain enablement, and no `dtbs_check` regressions for SoC DTSI files that include the provider topology.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,x1e80100-rpmh.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/qcom,x1e80100-rpmh.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm RPMh Network-On-Chip Interconnect on X1E80100` Qualcomm RPMh interconnect provider binding. RPMh interconnect providers support system bandwidth requirements through RPMh hardware accelerators known as Bus Clock Manager (BCM). The provider is able to communicate with the BCM through the Resource State Coordinator (RSC) associated with each execution environment. Provider nodes must point to at least one RPMh device child node pertaining to their RSC and each provider can map to multiple RPMh resources. See also: include/dt-bindings/interconnect/qcom,x1e80100-rpmh.h It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 19 compatible tokens: `qcom,x1e80100-aggre1-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-aggre2-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-clk-virt`, `qcom,x1e80100-cnoc-cfg`, `qcom,x1e80100-cnoc-main`, `qcom,x1e80100-gem-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-lpass-ag-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-lpass-lpiaon-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-lpass-lpicx-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-mc-virt`, `qcom,x1e80100-mmss-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-nsp-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-pcie-center-anoc`, `qcom,x1e80100-pcie-north-anoc`, `qcom,x1e80100-pcie-south-anoc`, `qcom,x1e80100-system-noc`, `qcom,x1e80100-usb-center-anoc`, `qcom,x1e80100-usb-north-anoc`, `qcom,x1e80100-usb-south-anoc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 2 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources `reg`, provider cells none, clocks/power/OPP hooks none, and SoC/vendor topology knobs none. Required properties are `compatible`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `qcom,rpmh-common.yaml#`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `else`, `if`, `then`.

Maintainers: Rajendra Nayak <quic_rjendra@quicinc.com>, Abel Vesa <abel.vesa@linaro.org>. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 2 top-level properties and 2 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/samsung,exynos-bus.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/samsung,exynos-bus.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Samsung Exynos SoC Bus and Interconnect` Samsung Exynos bus/interconnect binding. The Samsung Exynos SoC has many buses for data transfer between DRAM and sub-blocks in SoC. Most Exynos SoCs share the common architecture for buses. Generally, each bus of Exynos SoC includes a source clock and a power line, which are able to change the clock frequency of the bus in runtime. To monitor the usage of each bus in runtime, the driver uses the PPMU (Platform Performance Monitoring Unit), which is able to measure the current load of sub-blocks. The Exynos SoC includes the various sub-blocks which have the each AXI bus. The each AXI bus has the owned source clock but, has not the only owned power line. The power line might be shared among one more sub-blocks. So, we can divide into two type of device as the role of each sub-block. There are two type of bus devices as following:: - parent bus device - passive bus device Basic... It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 1 compatible token: `samsung,exynos-bus`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `devfreq`, `devfreq-events`, `exynos,saturation-ratio`, `#interconnect-cells`, `interconnects`, `operating-points-v2`, `opp-table`, `samsung,data-clock-ratio`, `vdd-supply`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 12 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `clock-names`, `clocks`, `compatible`, `operating-points-v2`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interconnect-facing properties define a provider node for the Linux interconnect framework and related devfreq/OPP consumers: addressing resources none, provider cells `#interconnect-cells`, clocks/power/OPP hooks `clocks`, `clock-names`, `operating-points-v2`, `opp-table`, `samsung,data-clock-ratio`, `vdd-supply`, and SoC/vendor topology knobs `#interconnect-cells`, `interconnects`, `operating-points-v2`. Required properties are `compatible`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `operating-points-v2`; consumer nodes depend on these cells and phandles to vote bandwidth or performance state.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For interconnect providers, validation ensures that provider cells, register banks, clocks, power domains, BCM voter links, and OPP or devfreq relationships are well-formed before Linux interconnect providers register bandwidth paths consumed by other device nodes. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are none.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Chanwoo Choi <cw00.choi@samsung.com>, Krzysztof Kozlowski <krzk@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 2 example blocks. Runtime dependencies commonly include the Linux interconnect framework, SoC-specific provider drivers, clock and power-domain providers, OPP/devfreq policy, and bandwidth consumers expressed by phandles.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 12 top-level properties and 12 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interconnect/samsung,exynos-bus.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `samsung,exynos-bus`. The 2 example blocks in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include successful interconnect provider registration, consumer bandwidth votes, devfreq/OPP transitions, clock and power-domain enablement, and no `dtbs_check` regressions for SoC DTSI files that include the provider topology.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/abilis,tb10x-ictl.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/abilis,tb10x-ictl.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `TB10x Top Level Interrupt Controller` interrupt-controller binding. The Abilis TB10x SOC contains a custom interrupt controller. It performs one-to-one mapping of external interrupt sources to CPU interrupts and provides support for reconfigurable trigger modes. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `abilis,tb10x-ictl`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupts`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 5 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `#interrupt-cells`, `compatible`, `interrupt-controller`, `interrupts`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interrupt-controller-facing properties define how child devices encode interrupt specifiers and how the kernel irqchip or MSI domain driver maps them: addressing resources `reg`, domain declarations `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupts`, clock/reset/power hooks none, and any GPIO or parent-domain links none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupts`; the shape of `#interrupt-cells` and parent interrupt wiring is the main ABI exposed to DTS consumers.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For irqchip and MSI providers, validation ensures the interrupt domain, specifier cell count, parent interrupt lines, MSI doorbells, and child-node encoding match what the kernel interrupt-controller driver will parse during early boot or bus enumeration. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are none; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are none.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Christian Ruppert <christian.ruppert@abilis.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree schema meta-schema, common bindings referenced through `$ref`, DTS/DTSI board files in the Ceph-client kernel tree, and subsystem drivers that match the compatible strings. Pattern properties are none; the file provides 1 example block. Runtime dependencies commonly include early irqchip initialization, hierarchical interrupt domains, MSI/MSI-X domains where present, parent interrupt controllers, and bus/device nodes that consume the domain through `interrupts`, `interrupts-extended`, or MSI-parent links.

## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. This schema has 5 top-level properties and 5 distinct nested property names, with strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users before edits, because a schema-only patch can still break board builds.

## Test Signals
Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/abilis,tb10x-ictl.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `abilis,tb10x-ictl`. The 1 example block in this file should compile under dt-schema and remain representative of the required properties. Useful integration signals include irqchip/MSI domain registration during boot, correct parent IRQ routing, valid child interrupt specifier lengths, interrupt delivery tests from devices below the domain, and no early-boot warnings about missing cells, ranges, or register resources.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/abilis,tb10x-ictl.yaml -->

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/amlogic,meson-gpio-intc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/amlogic,meson-gpio-intc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Amlogic Meson GPIO interrupt controller` ARM interrupt-controller binding. Meson SoCs contains an interrupt controller which is able to watch the SoC pads and generate an interrupt on edge or level. The controller is essentially a 256 pads to 8 or 12 GIC interrupt multiplexer, with a filter block to select edge or level and polarity. It does not expose all 256 mux inputs because the documentation shows that the upper part is not mapped to any pad. The actual number of interrupts exposed depends on the SoC. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/andestech,plicsw.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/andestech,plicsw.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Andes machine-level software interrupt controller` interrupt-controller binding. In the Andes platform such as QiLai SoC, the PLIC module is instantiated a second time with all interrupt sources tied to zero as the software interrupt controller (PLIC_SW). PLIC_SW directly connects to the machine-mode inter-processor interrupt lines of CPUs, so RISC-V per-CPU local interrupt controller is the parent interrupt controller for PLIC_SW. PLIC_SW can generate machine-mode inter-processor interrupts through programming its registers. It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses ordered `items` sequence and covers 2 compatible tokens: `andestech,qilai-plicsw`, `andestech,plicsw`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `compatible`, `interrupts-extended`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interrupt-controller-facing properties define how child devices encode interrupt specifiers and how the kernel irqchip or MSI domain driver maps them: addressing resources `reg`, domain declarations `interrupts-extended`, clock/reset/power hooks none, and any GPIO or parent-domain links none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts-extended`; the shape of `#interrupt-cells` and parent interrupt wiring is the main ABI exposed to DTS consumers.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. For irqchip and MSI providers, validation ensures the interrupt domain, specifier cell count, parent interrupt lines, MSI doorbells, and child-node encoding match what the kernel interrupt-controller driver will parse during early boot or bus enumeration. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/apple,aic.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/apple,aic.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Apple Interrupt Controller` interrupt-controller binding. The Apple Interrupt Controller is a simple interrupt controller present on Apple ARM SoC platforms, including various iPhone and iPad devices and the "Apple Silicon" Macs. It provides the following features: - Level-triggered hardware IRQs wired to SoC blocks - Single mask bit per IRQ - Per-IRQ affinity setting - Automatic masking on event delivery (auto-ack) - Software triggering (ORed with hw line) - 2 per-CPU IPIs (meant as "self" and "other", but they are interchangeable if not symmetric) - Automatic prioritization (single event/ack register per CPU, lower IRQs = higher priority) - Automatic masking on ack - Default "this CPU" register view and explicit per-CPU views This device also represents the FIQ interrupt sources on platforms using AIC, which do not go through a discrete interrupt controller. IPIs may be performed via MMIO r... It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses ordered `items` sequence and covers 7 compatible tokens: `apple,s5l8960x-aic`, `apple,t7000-aic`, `apple,s8000-aic`, `apple,t8010-aic`, `apple,t8015-aic`, `apple,t8103-aic`, `apple,aic`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, `reg`, `power-domains`, `affinities`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 8 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `#interrupt-cells`, `apple,fiq-index`, `compatible`, `cpus`, `interrupt-controller`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interrupt-controller-facing properties define how child devices encode interrupt specifiers and how the kernel irqchip or MSI domain driver maps them: addressing resources `reg`, domain declarations `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, clock/reset/power hooks `power-domains`, and any GPIO or parent-domain links none. Required properties are `compatible`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupt-controller`, `reg`; the shape of `#interrupt-cells` and parent interrupt wiring is the main ABI exposed to DTS consumers.

Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. ## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/arm,gic-v5.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `ARM Generic Interrupt Controller, version 5` ARM interrupt-controller binding. The GICv5 architecture defines the guidelines to implement GICv5 compliant interrupt controllers for AArch64 systems. The GICv5 specification can be found at https://developer.arm.com/documentation/aes0070 The GICv5 architecture is composed of multiple components: - one or more IRS (Interrupt Routing Service) - zero or more ITS (Interrupt Translation Service) The architecture defines: - PE-Private Peripheral Interrupts (PPI) - Shared Peripheral Interrupts (SPI) - Logical Peripheral Interrupts (LPI) It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `arm,gic-v5`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interrupt-controller-facing properties define how child devices encode interrupt specifiers and how the kernel irqchip or MSI domain driver maps them: addressing resources `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `ranges`, domain declarations `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupt-controller`, `interrupts`, clock/reset/power hooks none, and any GPIO or parent-domain links none. Required properties are `compatible`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `ranges`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupt-controller`; the shape of `#interrupt-cells` and parent interrupt wiring is the main ABI exposed to DTS consumers.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence

The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/interrupt-controller.yaml#`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint16-array`; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points

Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/arm,gic.yaml

`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/arm,gic.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `ARM Generic Interrupt Controller v1 and v2` ARM interrupt-controller binding. ARM SMP cores are often associated with a GIC, providing per processor interrupts (PPI), shared processor interrupts (SPI) and software generated interrupts (SGI). Primary GIC is attached directly to the CPU and typically has PPIs and SGIs. Secondary GICs are cascaded into the upward interrupt controller and do not have PPIs or SGIs. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/brcm,bcm2835-armctrl-ic.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/brcm,bcm2835-armctrl-ic.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `BCM2835 ARMCTRL Interrupt Controller` interrupt-controller binding. The BCM2835 contains a custom top-level interrupt controller, which supports 72 interrupt sources using a 2-level register scheme. The interrupt controller, or the HW block containing it, is referred to occasionally as "armctrl" in the SoC documentation, hence naming of this binding. The BCM2836 contains the same interrupt controller with the same interrupts, but the per-CPU interrupt controller is the root, and an interrupt there indicates that the ARMCTRL has an interrupt to handle. The interrupt sources are as follows: Bank 0: 0: ARM_TIMER 1: ARM_MAILBOX 2: ARM_DOORBELL_0 3: ARM_DOORBELL_1 4: VPU0_HALTED 5: VPU1_HALTED 6: ILLEGAL_TYPE0 7: ILLEGAL_TYPE1 Bank 1: 0: TIMER0 1: TIMER1 2: TIMER2 3: TIMER3 4: CODEC0 5: CODEC1 6: CODEC2 7: VC_JPEG 8: ISP 9: VC_USB 10: VC_3D 11: TRANSPOSER 12: MULTICORESYNC0 13: MULTICORESYNC1 14: MULTICORES... It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 2 compatible tokens: `brcm,bcm2835-armctrl-ic`, `brcm,bcm2836-armctrl-ic`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupts`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`; the shape of `#interrupt-cells` and parent interrupt wiring is the main ABI exposed to DTS consumers.

Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. The schema itself stores no mutable state and writes no persistent data; its persistence is the stable source-controlled hardware ABI. External references used in evaluation are none; top-level/nested conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `else`, `if`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Florian Fainelli <florian.fainelli@broadcom.com>, Raspberry Pi Kernel Maintenance <kernel-list@raspberrypi.com>. ## Risks
Risks are mostly ABI and validation risks rather than algorithmic failures. Tightening required properties, changing compatible ordering, renaming resources, altering cell counts, or closing extra properties can reject existing DTS users; loosening constraints can allow nodes that later fail driver probe or misdescribe hardware. Primary validation is `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/brcm,bcm2835-armctrl-ic.yaml` plus `make dtbs_check` on in-tree boards using `brcm,bcm2835-armctrl-ic`, `brcm,bcm2836-armctrl-ic`. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/brcm,bcm6345-l1-intc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/brcm,bcm6345-l1-intc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Broadcom BCM6345-style Level 1 interrupt controller` interrupt-controller binding. This block is a first level interrupt controller that is typically connected directly to one of the HW INT lines on each CPU. Key elements of the hardware design include: - 32, 64 or 128 incoming level IRQ lines - Most onchip peripherals are wired directly to an L1 input - A separate instance of the register set for each CPU, allowing individual peripheral IRQs to be routed to any CPU - Contains one or more enable/status word pairs per CPU - No atomic set/clear operations - No polarity/level/edge settings - No FIFO or priority encoder logic; software is expected to read all 2-4 status words to determine which IRQs are pending If multiple reg ranges and interrupt-parent entries are present on an SMP system, the driver will allow IRQ SMP affinity to be set up through the /proc/irq/ interface. In the simplest possible configuration, only... It is not executable kernel code; it is the hardware-description ABI that DTS authors, dt-schema, and the relevant kernel subsystem rely on so the runtime driver can parse the node without board-specific ambiguity.

Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 5 distinct property names; nested/all required properties include `#interrupt-cells`, `compatible`, `interrupt-controller`, `interrupts`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Interrupt-controller-facing properties define how child devices encode interrupt specifiers and how the kernel irqchip or MSI domain driver maps them: addressing resources `reg`, domain declarations `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupts`, clock/reset/power hooks none, and any GPIO or parent-domain links none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupt-controller`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupts`; the shape of `#interrupt-cells` and parent interrupt wiring is the main ABI exposed to DTS consumers.

Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation rather than executable branching. `dt-doc-validate` and `dt_binding_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, apply composition and conditional keywords, validate the DTS example blocks, and later `dtbs_check` applies the same contract to compiled board devicetrees. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/brcm,bcm7120-l2-intc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/interrupt-controller/brcm,bcm7120-l2-intc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Broadcom BCM7120-style Level 2 and Broadcom BCM3380 Level 1 / Level 2` interrupt-controller binding. This interrupt controller hardware is a second level interrupt controller that is hooked to a parent interrupt controller: e.g: ARM GIC for ARM-based platforms. It can be found on BCM7xxx products starting with BCM7120. Such an interrupt controller has the following hardware design: - outputs multiple interrupts signals towards its interrupt controller parent - controls how some of the interrupts will be flowing, whether they will directly output an interrupt signal towards the interrupt controller parent, or if they will output an interrupt signal at this 2nd level interrupt controller, in particular for UARTs - has one 32-bit enable word and one 32-bit status word - no atomic set/clear operations - not all bits within the interrupt controller actually map to an interrupt The typical hardware layout for this controller is represented...
